# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-27

## RAG
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[From One Query to Many: A Critical Survey of Privacy Accounting in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21245v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enables large language models to leverage external knowledge but introduces privacy risks when the retrieved corpus contains sensitive information. Differential privacy (DP) offers formal protection, yet interpreting and comparing privacy guarantees across existing DP-RAG methods remains challenging. In particular, a privacy budget reported for a single response does not directly characterize privacy loss over repeated queries, and even identical values of $\varepsilon$ may represent different guarantees depending on the protected data unit, accounting horizon, and observable outputs. This survey critically examines how privacy accounting evolves from single-query generation to multi-query RAG systems. We categorize existing approaches into query-time privatization, multi-query accounting, and one-time corpus privatization. We analyze their underlying assumptions and privacy--utility trade-offs. Rather than ranking methods by $\varepsilon$ alone, we propose a framework that interprets a DP-RAG guarantee along three dimensions: privacy granularity, accounting scope, and release boundaries. We conclude that an $\eps$ is a claim only together with the three slots: the protected unit, the accounting scope, and the release boundary.</p></details> |  |
| **[Is Complex Agentic Memory Always Needed? Naive RAG Is Stronger Than We Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30755v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study Raw-RAG, a naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to a reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, Raw-RAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmark-model settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.13-5.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>62 pa...</summary><p>62 pages, 5 figures. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[Compiling Autoregressive LLM Control into Policy-Exact Parallel Inference for Hierarchical RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30664v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models increasingly act as controllers in structured decision pipelines, yet their decisions are still executed by autoregressive text decoding. Hierarchical RAG is one such setting: at each frontier of a document tree, an LLM commits to $b$ of $k$ sections under an input-dependent action grammar, executed as either failure-prone free-form JSON or reliable but strictly serial grammar-constrained generation. We show that this serialization is unnecessary for bounded-horizon control: the reachable future forms a shallow action-prefix tree, which we compile --- one root prefill plus one tree-causal probe evaluate every prefix-conditioned action distribution with serial decoding's tokens, positions, and causal ancestry. A prefix-state lemma makes compiled control policy-exact in exact arithmetic: the same policy with a two-forward critical path and zero output tokens. One further probe pass certifies the exact order-marginalized MAP set. Across four document-disjoint splits and two scales on the primary MetaX device, compiled control matches the serial policy on $84$--$89\%$ of questions --- every instrumented divergence a floating-point near-tie --- and cuts control latency by $35$--$47\%$ versus token-wise serial decoding and $6$--$7\%$ versus an idealized chunked engine; an H800 repetition preserves the ordering.</p></details> | <details><summary>5 pag...</summary><p>5 pages, 2 figures, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: multidimensional collections whose inner lengths vary and are revealed only at runtime. Ragged data is prevalent in domains throughout science and computing, yet existing workflow engines treat it as nested containers. This representation leaves shape semantics ambiguous, obscures task dependencies when upstream coordinates are not yet enumerated, and imposes coarse synchronization barriers that stall independent work across tasks. We argue that ragged data is fundamentally a flat function over a coordinate space, and that making this view explicit resolves all three difficulties at once. We realize this insight in Operon, a statically verifiable workflow engine built around a formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Users declare pipelines in the Operon DSL by annotating data flow with dimension names, where the static checker verifies that every task does not include faulty iterations regardless of runtime lengths. At runtime, the system models in-progress arrays as partial functions over downward-closed coordinate domains, which provides a natural representation for partially-unknown data states, an exact notion of per-task indegree before all upstream coordinates are enumerated, and a sound basis for fault recovery without programmer intervention.</p></details> |  |
| **[From prompting to evidence-based translation: A RAG+prompt system for Japanese-Chinese translation and its pedagogical potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03387v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models perform well on high-resource pairs but are less reliable for Japanese-Chinese sentences containing noun-modifying clause constructions (NMCCs). This study evaluates a retrieval-augmented generation RAG+Prompt translation system that integrates linguistic analysis, embedding-based retrieval, prompt construction, and LLM generation without modifying the base model. The analysis module outputs A1 (inner vs. outer NMCC) and A2 (risk predictions: lexical choice/NMCC handling/word order/style/register); top-k = 5 similar Ja-Zh examples (L2 distance) and A1/A2 are inserted into an enhanced prompt. Using GPT-4o and a 66-sentence test set, we compare six knowledge-base sizes (0/100/200/500/1,000/2,000). Macro-averaged sentence-level BLEU (1-4-gram with brevity penalty; cased; Chinese at the character level) is the sole metric. Mean BLEU increases from 24.28 at 0 (RAG disabled) to 29.96 at 2,000 (+5.68; +23.4%). The upward trend holds across sizes, with larger knowledge bases yielding higher scores. We conclude that the RAG+Prompt translation system improves Ja-Zh translation of sentences containing NMCCs in an interpretable and auditable manner. Limitations include one base model, one metric, and reliance on published texts and commercial APIs; future work will broaden genres, language pairs, and evaluation metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>The a...</summary><p>The authors have identified errors in the experimental data and have decided to withdraw this version of the manuscript</p></details> |
| **[Protection Is Not One Event: A Survey of RAG Protection Research by Measured Endpoint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30193v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems face security, privacy, and copyright risks including content leakage, source reconstruction, unauthorized access, data poisoning, and prompt injection. Yet RAG protection studies use incompatible evaluation metrics, datasets, and threat models. We present a systematic survey and human-coded evidence map of 118 full-text-confirmed RAG defense papers, organized by measured endpoint rather than claimed goal or defense mechanism. Our seven-family taxonomy covers content disclosure, formal privacy proxies, access control and suppression, corpus-use detection, and pipeline integrity and attribution. Thirty-two annotators double-coded the papers kappa=0.867. The survey identifies three gaps. First, retrieval reranking and context rewriting each span five or six endpoint families, so mechanisms do not identify protection outcomes. Second, only 17.8% of studies use content disclosure or reconstruction as their primary endpoint; under the frozen copyright/ownership relation label, 73.3% of records have corpus-use detection as their primary endpoint, and none have an emitted-expression primary endpoint. Third, most evaluations use general-purpose question-answering datasets and non-adaptive attacks rather than sensitive data and adaptive, multi-turn extraction. Our controlled RAG testbed shows that preventing verbatim copying does not prevent private-fact recovery: a normalized 8-gram guard reduces 20-token copying to 0.0%, but a three-query adaptive attacker recovers 48.3% of hidden facts on Qwen3-8B. We release the taxonomy, dataset, coding protocols, and testbed to enable comparable, claim-aligned RAG security evaluation.</p></details> | <details><summary>ACL R...</summary><p>ACL Rolling Review (August 2026 cycle) recommends that the paper be accepted at an *ACL conference</p></details> |
| **[SIREN (Luring LLMs onto the Rocks): PAIR-Driven Preference Manipulation in Web-RAG Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21951v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates the adversarial manipulation of the ranked recommendations produced by web-augmented large language models (LLMs). When an LLM answers a recommendation query by retrieving and reading live webpages, it acts as a recommender, and each retrieved page becomes a potential attack surface. Prior work has examined fabricated products, retrieval poisoning, and rank promotion. However, these studies do not compare how different edits to an already retrieved page change the model's final ranking while the surrounding source set remains unchanged. To address this gap, we propose SIREN, an automated attacker--judge method that adapts the PAIR jailbreaking loop to competitive rank manipulation, with the goal of moving a chosen entity to rank~1 in an LLM-generated recommendation. SIREN retrieves and captures webpages using Anthropic's web tools, then iteratively edits a retrieved source using an interpretable taxonomy of 23 content-poisoning techniques. The custom-RAG replay platform keeps the same sources in the same order, so changes in the model's ranking can be linked to changes in the supplied content rather than to differences in retrieval. Across two production Claude models, SIREN reaches rank~1 in 62 of 124 technique trials nested within eight query--model contexts. The payloads that reached rank~1 were then tested in fresh sessions, where they reproduced the result with a mean success rate of 0.805. Across the evaluated settings, declarative ranking claims and seeded lists were generally more effective than directive-form injections, although the strength of this difference depended on the target model. To the best of our knowledge, this is among the first controlled studies of competitive rank manipulation in production LLMs where the supplied source context is kept fixed.</p></details> |  |
| **[Context-Aware Prompt Injection Detection via Small Language Models in RAG Pipelines: nokast-secureRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28364v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Local Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection and retrieval poisoning, where malicious instructions in retrieved content are mistaken for trusted input. We present nokast-secureRAG, a roughly 0.5B-parameter small language model that acts as a semantic firewall between retrieval and generation. It jointly evaluates the user query and retrieved context, classifying the pair as safe, suspicious, or malicious. The model is trained using a teacher-judge-student pipeline: a 35B teacher generates 5,233 examples across four attack families, and an independent 120B judge re-labels them, achieving 95.0% agreement before student fine-tuning. On a held-out in-distribution test set, the model achieves 0.994 detection recall at a 0.026 false-positive rate and a 0.006 attack-success-rate proxy, outperforming a regex baseline, a public prompt-injection classifier, Llama Guard 3 1B, and its untuned version. Prompt-Guard-86M reaches 0.900 recall but has a 0.690 false-positive rate. On a judgment-flip test, where the same sentence is benign or malicious depending on context, the model correctly classifies both cases 75% of the time, outperforming the compared baselines. Ablations indicate that retrieved context carries the main attack signal, while query-context consistency reduces false positives. Single-request latency is 293-404 ms, above the target of 100 ms, while amortized throughput latency is 37 ms under batching. On the external PoisonedRAG benchmark, recall falls to near zero, indicating that fluent fact-poisoning attacks lie outside the detector's instruction-consistency threat model. We report this limitation alongside the in-distribution results. The model, data-generation pipeline, and evaluation harness are released open source.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeclineCraft: A RAG Abstention Benchmark for Jointly Measuring Combined Retrieval and Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28340v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires recognizing when neither retrieved documents nor parametric knowledge supports an answer. We introduce DeclineCraft, a dynamic abstention benchmark whose unanswerable questions target Minecraft feature additions after a corpus cut-off, with audits of possible parametric exposure. Later cut-offs support evaluation of future models. Across 27 LLMs, we measure Correct Abstention Rate (CAR; how often models acknowledge insufficient knowledge on unanswerable questions), Answerable Attempt Rate and Answer Accuracy. We find that (1) single-shot retrieval significantly raises CAR in 38 of 40 configurations (model and thinking setting) versus no retrieval. (2) Iterative RAG, in which the model self-assesses its context and can request up to two further retrieval rounds, significantly lowers CAR versus single-shot RAG in 30 of 40 configurations (mean 46.4%), with little change in Answer Accuracy (68.5% to 69.3%). Adding query rewriting and reranking to hybrid (dense & sparse) retrieval lowers CAR for all 12 models tested. (3) Larger models answer more accurately (Spearman $ρ= +0.60$ with total parameters), but neither model size nor release date shows a significant association with CAR. (4) Without retrieval, enabling thinking on the same weights lowers CAR significantly for 8 of 13 models (by 14% points on average), and the responses shift from acknowledged uncertainty to premise denial.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 28 figures, 12 tables. Code: https://github.com/Thakur-Atharva/declinecraft-benchmark</p></details> |
| **[Probe, Don't Prompt: A Hidden-State Probe for Metadata Filtering in Multi-Meta-RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03929v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-Meta-RAG improves retrieval for multi-hop question answering by filtering a vector store on metadata, specifically the news source and the publication date, which it extracts from each query using GPT-3.5. This extractor requires a proprietary API call for every query, and its output is not restricted to the allowed sources: even though the prompt specifies 49 sources, GPT-3.5 generates 128 distinct source names, 79 of which do not appear on the list. We show that the source part of this extractor can be replaced by a local, deterministic probe trained on the hidden states of a small open-source language model. The probe is a multi-label logistic classifier on the mean-pooled hidden states of one layer, trained with a class-balanced loss on the gold evidence sources. Using 5-fold cross-validation to select the layer, we evaluate four models ranging from 135M to 1.5B parameters out-of-fold across all 2556 MultiHop-RAG queries. The probe reaches a set-exact accuracy of 90.9%, compared to 88.0% for a substring baseline and 80.9% for GPT-3.5. The difference comes from null queries, for which GPT-3.5 never predicts an empty source set. Across the remaining queries, all three methods achieve set-exact accuracies within 1.1 percentage points of each other. Unlike earlier probing work, we use the probe as a component of a retrieval pipeline rather than for interpretation. For all four models, the cross-validation selects one of the first layers, in contrast to the middle layers reported for retrieval tasks. We attribute this to the source name appearing verbatim in 95.4% of gold query-source pairs. The 135M model is 1.5 percentage points less accurate than the 1.5B model, so the filter requires only a partial forward pass through a small model and one linear layer. The code is available at https://github.com/mxpoliakov/Multi-Meta-RAG.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Computer Science and Applied Mathematics</p></details> |
| **[RAG-NIMBF: A Non-Invasive, Modular Benchmarking Framework for Automated RAG Configuration Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27816v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become the dominant paradigm for grounding the outputs of large language models (LLM) in external knowledge. However, practitioners are faced with a vast configuration space comprising chunking, embedding, retrieval, reranking, prompting and generation models, and there is limited guidance on how these components interact. We present RAG-NIMBF, a modular benchmarking framework that attaches to existing RAG pipelines as a non-invasive evaluation layer. It evaluates the configuration space through controlled ablation with end-to-end experiment tracking. The evaluation combines LLM-based answer quality scores, retrieval correctness measures, and runtime indicators. This study validates the framework across multiple generation models on the SQuAD dataset. This process reveals non-obvious interactions and demonstrates how the experiments are tracked. The findings indicate that the retrieval balance of relevance and diversity substantially enhances the relevance and coverage of retrieved context in comparison to a conventional similarity search, with MMR raising nDCG@5 by +4 to +11 percentage points across the swept models. More detailed prompt instructions meaningfully increase how faithfully generated answers stick to the retrieved evidence, especially for capable and reasoning models (+10 to +13pp on Qwen3.6-27B, GPT-4o-mini and Qwen3.5-think, but negligible on the 20B generator). Overall, RAG-NIMBF transforms the configuration space of RAG into reproducible, comparable experiments. By attaching to existing production pipelines in a non-invasive way, it enables practitioners to systematically evaluate and improve deployed systems without disrupting existing workflows. This replaces ad hoc choices with evidence-based decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Iterate Until Retrieved: Factual Nugget Optimization for Discoverable Continual Corrections in Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25641v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in business-to-business settings may often receive free-form response feedback. Rather than generic feedback signals such as style, preference, or overall response quality, we focus on actionable factual corrections. We identify these instances and convert them into compact knowledge-base entries, which we call factual nuggets. We introduce Iterative Nugget Optimization (INO), an index-time optimization method that uses the production agentic RAG as a test harness: it creates an initial nugget, probes it with the triggering query and paraphrases, reflects over failed retrieval and answer traces, and revises the nugget until it is discoverable. We evaluate INO with two production B2B knowledge-assistance agents across multiple companies that use our system: a product support agent that answers questions over company-specific knowledge bases, and a support ticket agent that assists support engineers. INO consistently improves results over baselines in terms of discoverability and usage of factual corrections, in automated and human evaluations. A controlled experiment in production shows that the nuggets improve user satisfaction with responses.</p></details> |  |
| **[Large-Scale Biomedical RAG: Corpus Scaling, Document Representation, and Efficiency Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26339v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present a controlled, large-scale evaluation of how corpus scale, document representation, and reranking affect the effectiveness and efficiency of biomedical Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Retrieval collections were progressively expanded while retaining the relevant source documents, enabling consistent comparisons across corpus sizes. We compared abstract, flat full-text, and hierarchical full-text representations across first-stage retrieval, reranking, and end-to-end multiple-choice question answering. Configurations selected on development data were evaluated on five external biomedical benchmarks and a held-out test set, while retrieval latency and index storage were measured to quantify operational cost. Performance patterns observed with small or controlled collections did not consistently persist as collections grew, and the usefulness of reranking depended on the corpus and retrieval setting. RAG improved three of four model configurations across the external benchmarks, with mean gains of up to 9.6 percentage points, and improved all four configurations on the held-out test set by 10.2-30.3 points. Abstract retrieval performed best when reference evidence was abstract-aligned, whereas full-text representations were generally more effective when richer evidence was needed. Hierarchical representation matched or outperformed flat full-text retrieval in most end-to-end comparisons while requiring 67% fewer indexed units and 44-56% less storage. These findings show that corpus scale should be treated as an explicit evaluation variable in biomedical RAG, and that the most appropriate design depends on evidence format, model behavior, and operational constraints.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 4 figures, 3 tables, plus 11 pages of supplementary material. Preprint submitted to Elsevier. Code: https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/biomedical-rag-scaling and https://github.com/hitz-zentroa/qa-to-mcqa</p></details> |
| **[StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26313v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code: github.com/SinghSuryaDeep/legal-rag-strucchunk-1</p></details> |  |
| **[Trust Before Fusion: QIMG-7 and Source-Aware Resolution for Polluted Multimodal RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10798v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is often evaluated with clean evidence, yet real retrieval can return topically relevant but unreliable content: false text and misleading images from corrupted metadata, entity swaps, typographic overlays, semantic edits, adversarial patches, blends, or style transfer. We introduce QIMG-7, a controlled benchmark for multimodal retrieval pollution in multi-sentence factual QA, spanning four datasets, seven image-attack families, and 16 paired clean/polluted regimes, for 1,760 evaluation rows per method. Across four generator/gate stacks, naive multimodal fusion is brittle: in the main gpt-4o-mini stack, Full-MM support drops from 0.908 with clean text to 0.490 with polluted text, often making Parametric fallback safer than retrieval. We propose source-aware trust resolution (SATR), a training-free approach that compares Parametric, Text-only, and Full-MM candidate answers and selects among candidate answers or falls back based on source reliability. The Field-Selector variant achieves the best balanced score, 0.816, improving over Full-MM by 11.7 points and over the Cascaded Router by 2.7 points. Ablations show that, in this text-first setting, explicit text-reliability modeling is the dominant driver of these gains. Overall, in text-first factual QA with multimodal retrieval conflict, our results support selective trust rather than unconditional fusion. Artifacts are available at https://github.com/SaadElDine/Trust_Before_Fusion.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 6 figures, 23 tables. Preprint under review</p></details> |
| **[The Bottleneck Is Retrieval, Not Reading: Diagnosing Evidence-Based Generation with RAG for Long Context Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26951v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is the dominant paradigm for grounding large language models in external corpora, but its similarity-driven, chunked pipeline has structural blind spots: it cannot anticipate which passages a question requires and discards document-level context that resolves cross-passage references. We introduce evidence-based generation (EBG), requiring models to return an answer with line-level citations, and a joint Ans+Evid metric that scores correctness and grounding simultaneously. We reformulate two corpora into evidence-grounded multi-document QA benchmarks- MultiHop-RAG and QASPER-MP, a multi-paper adaptation of QASPER with realistic short paper cues, and pair them with a controlled synthetic two-hop haystack and an evidence-augmented NoLiMa spanning 10K-1M characters. Across ten proprietary frontier and three open-weight LLMs, EBG isolates two RAG limitations that long-context models avoid: similarity retrieval surfaces any relevant chunk far more reliably than all required chunks for multi-fact queries, and chunking destroys document meta-information needed to resolve identity-bearing queries. EBG also exposes grounding failures invisible to answer-only evaluation, including incomplete citations in two-needle settings and a widening gap between Ans and Ans+Evid when context contradicts parametric knowledge. Data and code are available at https://github.com/debobanerjee/LC-EBG.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-RAG: Improving Secure Code Generation with Category-Aware Agentic Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24907v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite rapid progress in LLM-based code generation, security vulnerabilities remain widespread, and retrieval-augmented code generation (RACG) can further amplify risk when retrieved knowledge is verbose or vulnerable to poisoning. Existing secure RACG approaches inject security knowledge at a fine-grained vulnerability level, causing prompt overhead and diluted model attention when a query implicates multiple related weaknesses. We propose CAT-RAG, an agentic security category-aware retrieval-augmented code generation framework. CAT-RAG performs adaptive category selection via an LLM as a categorizer agent and operates either as a standalone security layer or as a drop-in add-on to RACG pipelines, requiring no modification to the underlying model. We replace CWE-level retrieval with compact, language-specific security categories. These categories provide a higher-level abstraction over individual CWE weaknesses, organizing them by the security concern they share, such as Input Validation and Injection Attacks, rather than merely grouping related entries. Each category consolidates best practices and secure code examples into a single, prompt-efficient representation. We perform a comprehensive evaluation across CyberSecEval, CodeGuard+, and HumanEval. On CyberSecEval, CAT-RAG nearly doubles the secure code generation improvement over the state of the art (23.57% vs. 12.11%) while reducing token overhead by 41.71%.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAG Strategies for Natural Language-Based SQL Query and REST API Call Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07086v3)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise software systems commonly expose functionality through relational databases and REST APIs, requiring users to understand schemas, endpoints, and parameters. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can support natural language interfaces by grounding large language models (LLMs) in enterprise-specific documentation. We evaluate three training-free RAG configurations - Standard RAG, Relevance Filtering RAG, and Iterative Retrieval RAG - for SQL query generation, REST API call generation, and a combined task requiring routing between both operation types. Using SAP Transactional Banking, we constructed an expert-reviewed dataset with mock execution screening and compared the configurations under database-only, API-only, and mixed-documentation settings. Without retrieval, exact match accuracy is 0% across all tasks, whereas retrieval achieves mock execution success of up to 79.30% and component match accuracy of up to 78.86%. In the combined task, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves higher exact match accuracy than Standard RAG (10.29% vs. 7.45%), with similar mock execution success (68.93% vs. 67.10%). However, for REST API call generation under mixed documentation, Iterative Retrieval RAG achieves lower mock execution success than Standard RAG (50.53% vs. 75.79%). Overall, retrieval-control strategies show task- and metric-dependent behavior under mixed-documentation settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the IEEE Proceedings of the 3rd International Conference on Foundation and Large Language Models (FLLM 2026), Barcelona, Spain, November 17-20, 2026</p></details> |
| **[VisR-RAG-Fin: Benchmarking Multimodal RAG over Visually Rich Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24822v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise documents such as financial reports are increasingly processed by automated systems, yet they are primarily designed for human reading and exhibit complex visual structure, combining dense text with tables, charts, diagrams, and rich layouts. These characteristics pose significant challenges for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, which must retrieve relevant pages and leverage visual and structural information during answer generation. Despite recent advances in multimodal retrieval and vision-language models, there remains a limited understanding of how design choices across retrieval and generation components affect end-to-end RAG performance on visually rich documents. In this paper, we present a comprehensive empirical study of multimodal RAG for page-level Question Answering (QA) over financial documents. We introduce VisR-RAG-Fin (Visually Rich RAG for Finance), an open-source benchmark constructed through a general pipeline for visually rich documents that preserves original layout and visual content. Using this benchmark, we systematically analyze retrieval and generation behavior under text-only, multimodal, and hybrid configurations, providing a foundation for rigorous evaluation of RAG systems on complex enterprise document collections. Overall, this work provides actionable insights for practitioners designing and evaluating multimodal RAG pipelines over complex, visually rich enterprise documents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the main research track of ACM SIGIR-AP 2026, Singapore, December 13-15, 2026</p></details> |
| **[RAGit: Turning Document Preprocessing into an Experimental Variable for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24659v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document preprocessing choices can substantially affect retrieval in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines, yet they are often selected heuristically and are difficult to compare under consistent conditions. We present RAGit, an open-source experimentation workbench for the systematic and reproducible evaluation of document preprocessing choices through downstream retrieval. RAGit represents parsing, chunking, indexing, retrieval, and evaluation as configurable experimental stages, enabling controlled comparisons while the remaining downstream settings are kept fixed. It preserves provenance from retrieved chunks to source pages and maps heterogeneous retrieval units to a common page-level ranking for evaluation. The current implementation supports multiple parsers and chunking strategies, dense and late-interaction retrieval, reusable intermediate artifacts, and multi-configuration benchmarking. We demonstrate RAGit through controlled experiments comparing alternative preprocessing configurations under a fixed retrieval setup, illustrating how preprocessing can be treated as an explicit experimental variable. RAGit and its documentation are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/OmarElbachyr/ragit). A demonstration video is available at https://youtu.be/tsst-Bhfz4I.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted at the Tool Demonstration Track of the 33rd Asia-Pacific Software Engineering Conference (APSEC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. | **[OntoAR-HAR: Ontology-Guided Agentic RAG with Personalized Memory for Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15292v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs increasingly support sensor-based human activity recognition (HAR) by linking motion signals with activity semantics and physical knowledge. However, existing LLM/RAG-based HAR typically searches single-level activity spaces using static population knowledge. Consequently, they compare many unrelated candidates using the same static population evidence and overlook personalized motion patterns. We propose OntoAR-HAR, an ontology-guided agentic RAG framework combining static population and personalized knowledge. Its tree-structured activity ontology decomposes classification into local decisions among semantically related activities. The static store supplies node-constrained examples, while offline-discovered executable signal features provide numerical evidence for local distinctions. During inference, the personalized store collects selected user observations, which the LLM integrates with static knowledge and signal features while traversing the ontology to an activity label. Cross-subject and heterogeneous-sensing experiments show complementary gains from hierarchical retrieval and personalized memory across multiple LLM backbones, achieving state-of-the-art performance. By explicitly modeling hierarchical relations across activity granularities, OntoAR-HAR also addresses the long-overlooked semantic inclusion problem and avoids treating related labels as independent categories.</p></details> |  |
| **[Retrieving Complementary Evidence for Multi-Hop RAG with Semantic Gain Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15285v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires finding documents whose evidence is useful in combination. Offline, a large language model assesses the relevant and nonredundant information contributed by one chunk given another within neighborhoods proposed by semantic similarity. These ordered assessments are aggregated into symmetric edge weights to construct a reusable Semantic Gain Graph. Online, controlled Personalized PageRank starts from query-relevant seeds and ranks complementary evidence beyond the initial retrieval set. Experiments on MuSiQue, 2Wiki, and HotpotQA with two language model backbones show that SG$^2$RAG achieves the best average EM, F1, and Recall@10 among the evaluated single-retrieval methods. Under identical candidate support and propagation, semantic gain improves average F1 and Recall@10 over similarity weighting by 4.08 and 4.31 points. Analyses of supporting documents and improvements in three iterative retrieval frameworks further support its effectiveness as a reusable retrieval component.</p></details> |  |
| **[SAFE-KV: Causal Suffix Repair for Behavior-Preserving Non-Prefix KV Cache Reuse in RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15186v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) reuses the same documents across many requests, making non-prefix KV-cache reuse attractive. On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, prefix-conditioned composition preserves only 47.2% of HotpotQA-500 and 37.8% of 2Wiki-500 full-prefill answers; SAFE-KV with risk-guided K2 selection restores 99.8%/98.4% (donor-copy protocol) and 100.0%/98.4% with actual suffix forwarding. Document-only and fixed-window repairs recover far less, because the document states after the repaired region keep histories that omit it. Reuse without repair is exact up to four documents and degrades at sixteen, where suffix repair recovers 98-100%. Faithful repair re-encodes most of the context, so in a single-request L40S study SAFE-KV's operating rule serves every workload by full prefill. SAFE-KV thus establishes the causal-state contract for non-prefix reuse and a validated repair mechanism that meets it.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Findings of AACL-IJCNLP 2026. 36 pages, 5 figures, 33 tables. Code: https://github.com/XingjianZhang-dev/safe-kv</p></details> |
| **[Post-Generation Verification Dominates Retrieval Optimization: A 2^4 Factorial Ablation of RAG Pipeline Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35774v3)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern RAG pipelines stack many enhancement features, but these features are typically validated in isolation, leaving their interactions unmeasured. The design crosses 16 configurations, 24 queries spanning eight interaction types, and two cloud-class models (768 designed runs, 759 completed and scored) on five public documents (78-492 pages), and every answer is scored against a verified reference. On this corpus and task, post-generation verification dominates: CC is the strongest feature (d=+0.48, p<0.001), improving accuracy, completeness, and usefulness simultaneously, and CC alone (4.31/5) is ahead of every configuration without it, including the three-feature SE+AS+ToC (4.11). ToC yields a significant gain at zero additional LLM calls (d=+0.22); AS shows a small, unstable effect that does not survive correction for multiple comparisons, helping some queries and harming others; SE is neutral. These main effects hold when every response is re-scored by two alternate LLM judges. The highest-quality configuration roughly doubles baseline latency, producing a genuine quality-latency Pareto frontier of six configurations. Feature utility is strongly query-type dependent (CC's completeness effect reaches d=+0.83 on completeness-demanding queries), so single-query-type evaluations systematically mis-rank features. | **[Training-Free BioNEL-RAG: Advancing Biomedical Entity Linking through Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14789v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical Named Entity Linking (BioNEL) is the task of mapping mentions extracted from biomedical texts to canonical entities or concepts in the standardized knowledge base. | **[Frustratingly Easy Agentic RAG via Portable Skills: A Case Study in Customer Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14428v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise workflow automation typically requires retrieving a complete and accurate set of relevant information from large operational document corpora (SOPs, policy documents) given a specific scenario or request. Meeting this bar in production typically requires complex document chunking, indexing, and retrieval pipelines, often layered with learned re-rankers, query rewriters, and bespoke agentic RAG infrastructure. We report a deployment case study of a simpler alternative: a portable skill consisting of a natural-language instruction, lightweight tools (a retrieval API, format-checking scripts), and a corpus enriched with an LLM-generated navigation layer, which is a structured "Wikipedia-style" index over the underlying documents. This agentic RAG approach is straightforward to implement (1-2 engineer days), portable across capable agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code), and performs strongly on the challenging task of CS resolution workflow generation, achieving 99.2% procedural equivalence to human-approved ground truth and 83.3% evidence sufficiency. It is also roughly an order of magnitude slower per case than our deployed production assistant (~90 s vs. ~8-9 s), so we claim no latency advantage. Our claim is scoped to that setting: a mature, well-curated corpus, a latency-tolerant workflow, and human review of every case. There, the skill did the work of a complex RAG stack, and maintenance became a matter of editing text and data rather than retraining models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 Industry Track. 15 pages, 1 figure, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[ORCAGen: Orchestrating Context-Aware Malware Deception with RAG-Guided Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12415v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Malware defenses often remove or isolate suspicious programs as quickly as possible. While effective for containment, this approach can also waste an opportunity to observe attacker behavior and deploy targeted countermeasures. ORCAGen takes a different approach: it uses GenAI to build malware-specific deception playbooks offline, validates them before deployment, and enforces only the verified logic at runtime. ORCAGen combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with structured prompt engineering to generate both proof-of-concept (PoC) malware and corresponding deception orchestration code. A curated knowledge base (KB) of malware procedures and active defense strategies grounds the generation process, helping the LLM produce threat-specific and executable deception logic rather than generic or hallucinated outputs. The generated PoC malware provides a safe and reproducible way to test whether a deception strategy can disrupt, redirect, or suppress targeted malware behavior before the strategy is added to the runtime playbook. We evaluate ORCAGen across GPT-4o, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Qwen3-Coder, and Claude Sonnet 4.5 using execution success, hallucination rate, refinement effort, deception effectiveness, and runtime overhead. The evaluation covers synthesized malware scenarios and 150 real-world malware samples across keyloggers, information stealers, and ransomware. Across the evaluated scenarios, GPT-5.5 required the fewest refinements and produced no observed hallucinated APIs, while Gemini 3.5 Flash achieved the lowest response time and runtime overhead. The results show that RAG-guided structured prompting can support the scalable construction of malware-specific deception playbooks that remain lightweight and deterministic during runtime enforcement.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at 2026 IEEE 8th International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems, and Applications (TPS-ISA)</p></details> |

Our findings suggest that finetuned GLM retrievers generalize better out of domain, achieving SOTA on two multi-hop benchmarks. GNN-based retrievers achieve higher graph coverage with an efficient training setup, whereas the vector-search baseline excels at single-hop datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026, 9 pages, 19 figures</p></details> |
| **[RAG-Stress: Probing the Limits of Evidence Reliance in Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11183v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following retrieved evidence does not guarantee factual correctness: misleading evidence can induce a model to replace an answer it previously gave correctly. The protocol holds the question and reference answer fixed, edits one assertion to support a designated incorrect answer, and crosses two source priority policies with three positions of the answer span within the evidence text. We measure misleading rate (MR) on each model's subset of questions answered correctly without retrieval, alongside clean accuracy on the full evaluation set. We evaluate fifteen systems spanning API models, open models, and search agents trained with reinforcement learning on TriviaQA-RC, HotpotQA, and SearchQA, with additional English and Chinese MedQA evaluations. Instructions that prioritize documents consistently produce higher MR than those permitting reliance on prior knowledge. Averaged over models and positions, the gap ranges from 10.9 to 13.5 percentage points across the three QA datasets. Mean MR follows End $>$ Beginning $>$ Middle under both policies, although individual models do not uniformly follow this ordering. A separate paired audit of 500 questions and two checkpoints supports increased harmful override without establishing a corresponding improvement in beneficial correction. These findings distinguish evidence adherence from factual reliability and motivate evaluating whether retrieved evidence preserves, replaces, or corrects a model's answers.</p></details> |  |
| **[Quantifying Retriever-Generator Alignment in RAG with Local Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21803v3)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems combine dense retrievers and language models to ground outputs in external documents. We show that WARG captures retriever-generator alignment better than Pearson and Spearman correlations and can serve as an indicator of RAG performance. RAG-E and WARG provide a practical framework for auditing this interaction, enabling more reliable and transparent RAG systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[EC-RAG: Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08674v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current large video-language models (LVLMs) still face challenges when dealing with long videos, mainly because frames are often processed independently, making it difficult to capture temporal dependencies across events. In this paper, we propose Event Chain Retrieval-Augmented Generation (EC-RAG), a training-free framework that organizes video content into an explicit event chain before question answering. Instead of retrieving isolated frames or text segments, EC-RAG first partitions the video into semantically coherent segments, represents each segment using multi-modal signals, and then links them into a structured chain that preserves temporal order and captures inter-event relationships. Given a query, the system identifies relevant events within this chain and gathers supporting evidence from the associated modalities. Our approach offers several practical advantages: (i) event-level abstraction that better reflects how video content is naturally structured, enabling more reliable localization compared to frame-level retrieval; (ii) structured multi-modal fusion that aggregates speech, text, and visual cues at the event level, allowing complementary information to be more effectively utilized during reasoning; and (iii) plug-and-play compatibility with existing LVLM backbones, requiring no additional training or reliance on proprietary models. Experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench show that this event-centric design consistently outperforms frame-level retrieval baselines, highlighting the importance of modeling temporal structure for long-video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 7 figures, 7 tables, including supplementary material</p></details> |

| **[Agentic AutoRAG: RAG Pipeline Optimization through Reasoning-Driven Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08452v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a widely used approach for grounding large language models (LLMs) in external knowledge. On three multi-hop QA benchmarks it reaches higher LLM-judge accuracy than every baseline we compare, and within its first 10 trials it matches or beats the statistical baselines' full 30-trial judge accuracy. Code: https://github.com/Agentic-Systems-Lab/Agentic-AutoRAG</p></details> |
| **[Tool-calling retrieval versus vector RAG for a small Greek--English knowledge base: accuracy and robustness to how users type Greek](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08205v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistants grounded in a small, frequently edited knowledge base can retrieve through tool calls to a live data interface or through vector retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). Letting the router write the vector query changed nothing, and placing the whole knowledge base of about 26,000 tokens in the prompt reached 99.3\%. The tool agent's losses arose in retrieval. Its literal searches returned nothing when the router's arguments did not occur verbatim in a record, for example when it transliterated Greek into Latin script or combined words that occur in a record but not as one phrase, and the agent then abstained. Unaccented and capitalised questions cost the tool agent about 20 points and vector RAG at most 2; accent-insensitive search removed this loss, and matching stemmed tokens raised the tool agent to 83.8\% on canonical Greek. | **[DrugMCTS: a drug repurposing framework combining multi-agent, RAG and Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07426v4)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in large language models have demonstrated considerable potential in scientific domains such as drug repositioning. The framework employs five specialized agents tasked with retrieving and analyzing molecular and protein information, thereby enabling structured and iterative reasoning. Our results highlight the importance of structured reasoning, agent-based collaboration, and feedback-driven search mechanisms in advancing LLM applications for drug repositioning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Knowledge Retrieval for Generation: A Survey on RAG Architectures and Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01936v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable fluency and versatility across natural language tasks but remain fundamentally limited by their static knowledge and susceptibility to hallucinations, especially in domains requiring up to date or attribute grounded information. We formalize the RAG framework through its three foundational components retrieval, generation, and augmentation and survey state of the art methods spanning dense and sparse retrievers, fusion strategies, embedding optimizations, and reinforcement learning based retrieval policies. Anchored around four emerging axes efficiency, security, user centric interactivity, and complex reasoning we categorize recent innovations and highlight their implications for scalability, robustness, and personalization. The paper also reviews advances in evaluation protocols, domain specific applications, and architectural variants such as Naïve RAG, Advanced RAG, and Modular RAG. Finally, we identify persistent challenges and outline future directions aimed at advancing the integration of retrieval with LLMs for more grounded, interpretable, and controllable generation.</p></details> |  |

Replaying decisions from a recipe-specific detector illustrated the protocol boundary: false promotions left poison visible, while false refusals excluded clean content from retrieval. Accepted at IEEE CBDCom 2026. Author accepted manuscript. Reproducibility artifact: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23152010</p></details> |

Normative retrieval involves a distinct asymmetry between context rich narrative queries and generalized normative statements. Existing factual retrieval methods rely on query-only expansion into a document-like form, making them insufficient for resolving this asymmetry. Therefore, we propose Intermediate Representation-based Embedding Alignment (IREA), a bidirectional alignment method that maps both text types into a shared situation-behavior representation. Experimental results show that IREA improves normative retrieval and downstream ethical judgment across multiple settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of bidirectional alignment for normative RAG.</p></details> |  |

Preprint</p></details> |

On the annotators' labels, the analysis did not establish a citation-support advantage for the wiki. | **[SceneBloom: Agentic Indoor Environment Generation with Realistic and Diverse Layouts for Embodied Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32978v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training generalizable embodied agents requires realistic, varied, and simulation-ready indoor environments. Language-guided methods support semantic planning and object-level reasoning, but precise spatial grounding remains difficult. Visual generative models offer rich priors over plausible arrangements, yet their outputs require geometric reasoning to become physically valid 3D scenes. We introduce SceneBloom, an agentic framework that combines visual layout sampling with contract-guided 3D compilation. A hierarchical sampler uses diffusion priors to generate furniture arrangements within functional zones. A compiler then uses relations extracted from each proposal to guide geometric fitting and local pose correction. Across five room types, SceneBloom attains the highest visual quality and physical stability among the compared methods and is the only method with no detected mesh collisions and out-of-bounds objects. Its layouts are also closer to the distribution of real rooms than those of all compared baselines. Object navigation policies trained in these realistic scenes achieve a higher success rate in held-out real scans than those trained on an equal number of real training scans. Code is available at https://github.com/Tinaliu0123/scenebloom.</p></details> |  |
| **[GaP: A Graph-as-Policy Multi-Agent Self-Learning Harness For Variational Automation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05369v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For robots to work reliably in commercial and industrial applications, recent advances in agentic coding systems have potential to combine interpretable robot programming with the open-world adaptability of model-free policies. Motivated by prior work on Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) and the Robot Operating System (ROS), we introduce Graph-as-Policy (GaP), a multi-agent coding harness that generates directed computation graphs with perception, planning, and control nodes from a Modular Open Robot Skill Library (MORSL). GaP generates an internal simulation environment to rehearse task instances with different graphs in parallel to iteratively refine the graph structure and parameters to improve success rates and throughput. Evaluation with 8 new open VA task benchmarks, 4 in simulation and 4 in real-world, suggests that GaP can achieve success rates that significantly outperform baselines. Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient. | **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lessons Outlive Their Failures: A Case Study of Rules for AI Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32578v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents increasingly work inside a harness: rule files such as AGENTS.md, on-demand skills, runtime hooks and automated checks. I report a six-week case study of my personal system of record, a plain-markdown repository that three AI agent tools edited daily under one shared rule set. I built a ledger of 118 agent failures, their fixes and whether they came back. I also tested the system's monthly LLM documentation audit against 47 known stale statements and 20 planted errors. Finally, I replayed five past failures in isolated git clones, with and without their fixes. All results come from one repository with three runs per condition, so I report them as exploratory. Stale documentation (drift) was the largest failure class (48 of 118), although 35 of those were found on one audit day. A single audit run found about half of the known drift (Claude Sonnet 5.5 mean: 50%), and at least 93% of its findings were correct. On Sonnet 5.5, the agent finished four of the five replayed tasks. In three of those four, the failure did not happen, with or without its fix. Only the stale-count failure came back without its check, and the check stopped it in every run. On older models, an agent drafted an em-dash once while the em-dash lint check was active, and the check rejected it. A written rule on run-time estimates did not change Sonnet 4.5's overestimates. These results suggest that harness lessons can outlive the failures they were written to fix, while drift persists and still needs mechanical checks. Tools that evolve a harness add lessons faster than they retire them, so they should re-test their rules from time to time and retire the obsolete ones.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Tool-Calling LLM Agents Under Realistic MCP Faults: Scale Does Not Purchase Fault Tolerance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32555v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools exposed via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), yet current benchmarks assume tools respond correctly and on time, leaving agent resilience to realistic tool failures untested. Code and data: https://gitlab.com/uniluxembourg/snt/sedan/papers/mcp-injector (archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23573765)</p></details> |

We call attacks seeking responses designed to resemble legitimate answers while introducing incorrect or harmful changes near-target prompt injection. Under a common budget of three attack executions per sample, both tracks achieve higher overall attack success rates than the fixed-target baseline for every evaluated model in each setting. On language tasks, the increases range from 9.3 to 35.8 percentage points for incorrect targets and 1.7 to 17.3 percentage points for harmful targets. | **[BeforeIT.jl: High-Performance Agent-Based Macroeconomics Made Easy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.13267v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BeforeIT is an open-source software for building and simulating state-of-the-art macroeconomic agent-based models (macro ABMs) based on the recently introduced macro ABM developed in [1] and here referred to as the base model. | **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. Recently, the meaning of this has changed dramatically as more and more AI agents now consume, retain, and act on information on a user's behalf. In this perspective paper, we argue that \emph{agentic memory management} (AMM), which studies the structure, analysis, organization, storage, searching, retrieval, and \emph{use} of information in an intelligent agent's memory, is a natural extension of IR research and an area in which the IR community can make distinctive contributions. Specifically, we first present a system-level framework for AMM and introduce a computational taxonomy of memory objects as \emph{constants}, \emph{functions}, and \emph{operators}, each with distinct retrieval semantics. Then, we compare AMM with established IR research and identify their difference and connections. Last but not least, we outline a research agenda covering benchmarks, candidate principles of memory utility, optimization procedures that mix parametric and non-parametric updates, and more. Among these tasks, at least 22.7 percent contain incorrect observations despite successful tool execution. We introduce a verifiable observation protocol that requires tools to return process evidence for the agent to verify their observations. The prior summarizes each tool configuration's past performance across task types and provides a task-specific reference for verification. Using process evidence and this prior, RSure-Agent decides whether to accept an observation, request additional evidence, or reject it. | **[Measuring the Energy Cost of Completing Tasks with Quantized Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32417v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantization reduces the memory and energy needed to serve large language models (LLMs) and AI agents for coding and data analysis can now run on locally hosted LLMs. In practice, this benefit is commonly benchmarked per output token. We trace this discrepancy to the extra tokens needed to complete a task in full, which translates into using up to 3.5 times as much energy per completed task. The gain is small and comes mostly from the sceptic call; we did not test whether its lower-only rule matters. Investments that later exceeded $2\times$ had 64% of factual categories corroborated, against 38% for write-offs (omnibus Kruskal-Wallis test over five outcome groups, $p=0.031$), and our retrieval was not date-restricted, so these figures may overstate screening skill. | **[Adaptive Control of Scientific Instruments with Nonstationary Responses through Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32370v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Controlling scientific instruments requires parameters to be adjusted progressively in response to feedback from successive operations. | **[DeepAVR: Deep Analysis via Multi-Agent Collaboration for Automated Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32345v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated Vulnerability Repair (AVR) has emerged as a critical defense against software security flaws, aiming to triage and fix bugs autonomously. We extract atomic claims from the CoT with an LLM, assess them against evidence gathered by an LLM agent, and manually trace the defects of incorrect patches back to faulty claims, confirming each link with a claim-corrected replay. Task-grounded reasoning covers only 18.5% of the CoT tokens, yet yields 191 atomic claims per trajectory, at least 9.6% of which are faulty. To understand why, we measure the gap with three quantities: background, the embedding component that does not separate skills; unsaid vocabulary, the gold skill's content a request leaves unsaid; and dilution, the information lost when a skill is encoded into a single vector. Code: https://github.com/EverMind-AI/SkillCorpus/tree/main/skillcorpus/lifecycle Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/EverMind-AI/skillcorpus-lifecycle</p></details> |

A minute of audio is millions of numbers, yet today's audio models hold conversations and write music. They "learned" to do this by "listening" to tokens. | **[Do AI-for-Science Agents Preserve Model Safety?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32045v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-for-Science agents are increasingly being used for formulating scientific hypotheses, running autonomous experiments, and interpreting evidence. | **[Train4Merge: RL or SFT---Which Yields Better Teachers for OPD-Based Model Merging?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32303v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Domain experts trained from a shared checkpoint can transfer their specialized capabilities to a single student through on-policy distillation (OPD). Existing research primarily focuses on improving this merging process, while the algorithms used to train the experts have received limited systematic comparison. We investigate which training algorithm produces teachers better suited to OPD through controlled single-teacher comparisons of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) across Agentic, Reasoning, and Perception. We further examine whether this advantage extends to multi-teacher OPD (MOPD). Teachers and students share the same Qwen3.5-9B initialization, and the two teacher types are compared at similar task performance. Our single-teacher experiments show that RL teachers yield stronger students and higher recovery of teacher performance gains across all three domains. In MOPD, the RL teacher group also yields a student with a higher three-domain mean score and higher overall teacher gain recovery than the SFT teacher group. Our further analysis shows that RL teachers undergo smaller parameter displacements from the shared initialization than SFT teachers. | **[SFT Leaves a Reproducible Signature in Activation Geometry: Calibrating Post-Training Interpretability Against Run-to-Run Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17093v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model diff credits post-training with whatever separates a base checkpoint from one post-trained descendant, presuming a rerun would land close by. We score each base-to-post contrast against the run-to-run band of its own recipe, and show that $k$ runs certify no better than $p=1/(k+1)$ under run exchangeability, however large the effect, so the band $z$-score is a descriptive scale. SFT leaves a large, reproducible signature: base $\to$ SFT displaces the graph by 17.8 pairwise-distance SDs beyond the mean distance between four independent LoRA-SFT runs, and the pre-registered estimator replicates on Llama-3.1-8B with Llama-Scope. At 29 steps each, four SFT and four DPO runs occupy disjoint displacement ranges, a gap $5.0\times$ and $5.6\times$ the spreads of the two arms, without any band. All bands use graphs built on one GPU architecture. A pre-registered causal sweep at three edge budgets on 4,816 held-out prompts found the displaced sources load-bearing ($12\times$ a matched random control in mean, $3.5\times$ in median at 10,000 edges), but their stage-specificity interaction reversed at two budgets and the third failed its faithfulness gate, so we claim calibrated structural displacement, not a changed mechanism. Scope: one backbone, one SFT recipe and general-text probe states for the full protocol.</p></details> |  |

PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. | **[What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. ## RLHF
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[CleanRM: Reward Modeling from Noisy Feedback with a Few Clean Feedback for RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback noise is pervasive in preference data, arising from subjective human judgments, annotation mistakes, and imperfect LLM judges. Standard reward modeling trained on such feedback yields a biased estimate of the clean risk. Although classical denoising methods are directly applicable to noisy feedback learning, they generally assume a uniform noise ratio across samples. However, this assumption fails for feedback data, where noise is intrinsically sample-wise. To address this challenge, we propose CleanRM, which uses a few clean feedback to calibrate sample-wise noise ratios via optimal transport. Specifically, CleanRM aligns the joint distribution of preferences reverted from noisy feedback with that of clean feedback and then trains the reward model using a noise-corrected surrogate loss. Theoretically, we prove that the resulting objective is unbiased with respect to the clean risk. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CleanRM outperforms state-of-the-art denoising baselines and yields more effective downstream RLHF alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rater State Bias in RLHF Preference Data: An Audit Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16195v3)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We identify a structured confound in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Pairwise preference labels are intended to reflect the compared outputs, but they may also reflect the rater's state during annotation. Under sustained stressful or distressing conditions, raters' preferences may shift over time, so that preference data encode rater state alongside judgments about response quality. We argue that, if present, such shifts would differ from ordinary disagreement or random label noise. They would be state dependent, could be shared across annotators under similar conditions, and would not necessarily cancel during aggregation, reward modeling, and policy optimization. We propose rater state shift as a plausible and testable source of structured bias in RLHF preference data. This paper develops a hypothesis and an audit framework for studying this source of bias. We define rater state shift, rater state confound, and correlated rater state bias. We also propose survival level emotional authenticity as a candidate output signature, defined by lexical, pragmatic, discourse, and safety features whose reliability and validity remain to be demonstrated. We show that systematic rater state bias can survive aggregation and may enter the learned reward signal. We state five testable predictions, together with effect size thresholds for an initial audit, and note which require proprietary data. Finally, we present an audit protocol and pilot study plan that can be applied to publicly available instruction tuned models. We do not infer the training history of any specific deployed model.</p></details> | <details><summary>v3: R...</summary><p>v3: Refined the mathematical analysis of bias propagation and policy optimization. Extended the discussion to model judges and the possible transmission of preference bias across models. Minor revisions throughout</p></details> |

To address this gap, we conduct a comprehensive study of prevalent reward-shaping techniques. Our analysis identifies two key design principles: (1) the reinforcement-learning reward should be bounded, and (2) it should grow rapidly at first and then gradually saturate. Motivated by these principles, we propose Preference as Reward (PAR), a novel method that uses the latent preferences encoded in the reward model as the reinforcement-learning signal. We further show that PAR possesses two variance-reduction properties that stabilize RLHF training and substantially widen the practical window for early stopping. Our evaluation consists of two parts. First, we compare PAR with several other reward-shaping strategies using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) as the reinforcement-learning algorithm and Gemma2-2B as the base model. Second, we compare PAR with the vanilla baseline (i.e., unshaped reward) across four base models and four reinforcement-learning algorithms. In the first set of experiments, PAR consistently outperforms other reward-shaping methods and also reflects high data efficiency and robustness. The second set of experiments shows that PAR is particularly effective for actor-critic RL algorithms when value estimates become unstable and demonstrates its effectiveness across different base models. The code is available at https://github.com/PorUna-byte/PAR.</p></details> |  |

But scoring and generation fight over the same CPU and GPU resources, so a faster scoring engine doesn't shrink step time on its own. It mainly frees up capacity generation can use instead. We built a native C++ inference engine on ONNX Runtime. First step: confirm correctness. Output matched the PyTorch reference to 5.7 x 10^-6 on CPU and 4.2 x 10^-3 on GPU, close enough to trust. Then we tested it against PyTorch eager mode, torch.compile, and FastAPI, on both CPU and GPU. CPU was decisive. Our engine beat every baseline, confidence intervals didn't even overlap. GPU gave a different view: we beat PyTorch and FastAPI, but torch.compile came out ahead. Further testing traced the speedup to ONNX Runtime itself, not C++ as a language. And batching strategy mattered more than either the language or the runtime choice, more than we expected. The results are from repeated, independent runs, since single runs just aren't reliable enough to trust.</p></details> |  |
