| **[Measure Before You Train: Typed Decision Models vs. a Classical Baseline vs. a Generative LLM for Routing a Personal Multilingual Inbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32955v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personal email agents decide, for every message, what it is, whether it is urgent, whether to draft a reply, create a task, or ignore it. Their error rate is rarely measured: the stored labels were written by the system itself. We present Foyer, a self-hosted personal inbox agent in daily use, and a measurement protocol (stratified sampling, frozen snapshots, thread-grouped splits, pre-registered hypotheses, human labels in the agent's review loop) that yields ground truth before any training. On 739 real Italian/English emails labelled by the owner (231 unbiased, 500 adversarially sampled), we compare nine systems under thread-grouped cross-validation: the production keyword rules, a TF-IDF + logistic-regression baseline, local typed decision models (Tev1, Nimble), a hosted decision model (Nimble v4-max), and hosted generative LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma 4 26B/31B). Four results. The production rules score 28% on message type against a 58% majority class: the field shown most prominently carried no information. With ~200 labels, logistic regression has the highest accuracy on every field (type 0.79, urgency 0.74, needs-reply 0.83, is-noise 0.90), though it never predicts the two rarest classes and is statistically tied with Gemma 4 on type; few-shot prompting does not close the gap. The sender's relationship (client, supplier, service) is recovered from other emails of the same contact with 98.6% accuracy where the contact has been seen, and is not inferable from a single message by any model: it belongs in the contact graph, not the prompt. Finally, accuracy is not the product metric: composed into actions, the most accurate system most often ignores emails needing action (18% vs 4% for the best LLM); a cost-chosen threshold on its noise probability halves that rate and makes it the cheapest system.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 tables. Single-user case study. Code and protocol: https://gitlab.com/federicolucca/foyer-measure . The emails are private and not released</p></details> |
| **[VETTA: Coordinating Turn- and Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08402v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents often receive sparse task feedback across several interactions, while generating each response token by token. This creates two related credit-assignment questions: which responses helped achieve the outcome, and which generation decisions mattered within each response? Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. These complementary limitations motivate learning credit at both levels and coordinating it in a single policy update. We introduce VETTA, a credit assignment method that jointly learns turn- and token-level values through separate heads on a shared lightweight critic. VETTA computes advantages along both temporal sequences and combines each turn advantage with a within-response-centered token residual for PPO updates. Furthermore, to reduce value-learning cost, the critic retains only early Transformer blocks from the pretrained checkpoint used to initialize the actor. Experiments on four agent benchmarks demonstrate VETTA's strong performance. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, VETTA achieves success rates of 95.5% on ALFWorld, 76.0% on WebShop, and 52.2% on ScienceWorld. With Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, it reaches 84.9% and 80.7% success on Sokoban under sparse and dense rewards, respectively. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiaju-Chen/VETTA-official.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Monetizing Intelligence: Advertising and Subscription Design on LLM Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32945v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) platforms introducing advertising must decide how much capability ad revenue should fund and what consumers should pay to avoid ads. We study a monopolistic platform that jointly chooses costly response quality, advertising intensity, and subscription prices when advertising reduces user value but better service raises advertising returns. Three findings follow. First, when partly ad-supported and ad-free plans coexist, advertising can finance useful service for consumers who receive none under pure subscription. Lower effective quality in middle plans weakens the alternatives available to premium users, allowing higher prices for unchanged ad-free service. Some fully ad-supported users gain utility, while partly ad-supported and ad-free users lose: broader access can coexist with utility polarization. Second, an intermediate advertising cap can maximize consumer surplus by preserving some revenue that finances service while limiting degradation. Third, cheaper compute can raise quality and reduce ad intensity, yet sufficiently large cost reductions can increase aggregate advertising revenue above its initial level because better service raises the value of the remaining advertised interactions. An extension shows how advertising-funded upgrades can bring plans to the same maximum capability and advertising exposure, eliminating the basis for different prices. These results make menu design central to evaluating LLM advertising: the consequences extend beyond users who see ads to the alternatives and prices faced by those who do not.</p></details> |  |
| **[Green Code or Costly Code? Evaluating the Energy Costs of Iterative LLM-Based Code Optimizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32891v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to optimize code for performance. This practice often assumes that improvements in execution speed naturally translate into overall lower energy consumption. We challenge this assumption empirically by measuring whether performance-optimized code produced by LLM self-refinement pipelines is also energy-efficient relative to canonical human reference solutions, and whether the energy cost of the optimization process can be offset by subsequent execution-time savings. We evaluate 11 open-source models across four decoding configurations on all 1,000 problems of EffiBench, a state-of-the-art performance benchmark, while collecting per-process, hardware-level energy measurements throughout. Inference energy spans 0.79 to 8.68 MJ across model-variant pairs, which is roughly equivalent to the energy of 250 to 2800 GPT-4.1 queries. Limiting the maximum number of output tokens has the largest impact, reducing energy use by up to 69% compared to the default configuration. On the execution side, 42 of 44 configurations produce code that consumes more energy per problem than the canonical human reference, with overheads ranging from +4% to +886%. The two exceptions achieve only marginal gains, with break-even points on the order of 10^6 to 10^7 executions. Mixture-of-Experts architectures consume substantially less inference energy than equivalently-sized dense models without sacrificing correctness, making architectural choice a more consequential sustainability lever than decoding parameters. Performance optimization and energy efficiency are not always aligned goals: LLM self-refinement consistently produces faster-seeking but energy-heavier code relative to what a human engineer would write. All artifacts are available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23598170.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 49th IEEE/ACM International Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE 2027)</p></details> |
| **[Advancing SVD-based LLM Compression via Layer-Wise Error Model Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32878v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-rank SVD-based compression offers a powerful strategy to reduce the computational costs of LLMs. However, existing methods face two key limitations: (i) global rank allocation, where uncalibrated error proxies fail to capture complex error propagation, and (ii) decomposition quality, where Fisher-based estimators suffer from severe rank collapse. In this work, we address these limitations by introducing Layer-wise Error Modeling Search (LEMS) and KFAC-SVD. LEMS advances rank allocation by introducing a layer-wise error surrogate that integrates local and global layer importance alongside a propagation bias, enabling effective global rank allocation via an ILP formulation. KFAC-SVD improves decomposition quality by utilizing token-wise statistics, mitigating the rank deficiency observed in prior Fisher-based SVD approaches. Across Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 model families, we show that LEMS consistently outperforms existing search strategies, delivering significant zero-shot accuracy gains of up to 4.8 p.p. that generalize to model sizes of 70B parameters, while KFAC-SVD achieves an average perplexity improvement of 15%. Project page and code: https://lems-svd.github.io</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ICML 2026</p></details> |
| **[Trusting the Tool: Asymmetric Deference of LLM Vulnerability Auditors to Static-Analysis Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32876v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pipelines that combine large language models (LLMs) with static analysis let the model consume the analyser's output as evidence, but whether the model distinguishes true from false evidence, and whether its scrutiny depends on what the evidence claims, has not been measured. We built an intraprocedural analyser for C/C++ that produces proof obligations and audited vulnerable and patched functions from PrimeVul under six evidence conditions that differ only in the notes, including fabricated assurances that a missing check exists and fabricated alarms that an added check is missing. The study covers 916 pairs in four disjoint sets, eleven model configurations from four vendors and 16,572 audits; no model is trained or fine-tuned. True evidence barely improved accuracy (+0.02). False evidence acted asymmetrically: relative to true notes about the same operations, false assurances lowered the detection of real vulnerabilities by up to 19 points, whereas false alarms never significantly lowered accuracy and were disputed far more often. An internally registered replication on seven new models confirmed that false assurances move verdicts towards their claim more than false alarms do (mean asymmetry +0.16, 95% CI +0.10 to +0.24). Instructions in the audit prompt did not reliably reduce deference; the best one failed to replicate. Verifying each claim in a separate call and showing the refuted claims raised accuracy under false assurances by 12 points in a pre-specified test (p=0.002) without loss under true evidence; for a second model the gain was smaller and not significant. Pipelines should therefore verify a tool's guarantees before the model relies on them, and studies should measure deference in a direction-aware way.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 5 tables. Submitted to IEEE Access. Code: https://github.com/lzh0082-cloud/CODE-DETECTION</p></details> |
| **[Do "True" and "False" Add Up to One? Complement Consistency in LLM-Agent Opinion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32874v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM-agent opinion simulations, each agent reports the probability that a claim is true after seeing its own previous probability and the probabilities reported by its neighbors, and that number becomes its state in the next round. If the number describes the agent's position rather than the sentence it was shown, then rewriting the claim as its complement and replacing every probability p with 1 - p should return the complementary answer, so the two reports should sum to one. We test this with three small open models: Qwen3-4B, Llama-3.2-3B and Gemma-3-4B. On 2,304 agent states taken from a running network simulation, Qwen's paired reports sum to 1.18 on average, and on 48 real fact-checking claims from AVeriTeC they again exceed one. A controlled benchmark of 24 worlds, each with exactly two possible outcomes, shows the same failure in all three models. The discrepancy is two to ten times larger than the change produced by a same-meaning paraphrase, and it persists under repeated sampling and across peer group sizes and report strengths. When the reports are fed back into two populations that start from complementary states, the populations do not remain mirror images: their divergence is positive after five rounds for every model. Averaging each answer with the complement of its paired answer restores consistency by construction and lowers Brier loss averaged over the two wordings, but at the final round it beats the better of the two original wordings for only one of the three models. A schedule-by-targeting experiment on the same model gives a small, stable interaction because such sweeps keep the claim wording fixed. Consistency under rewording therefore needs its own test before simulated probabilities are read as beliefs</p></details> |  |
| **[AI Alignment for Digital Democracy: Fairer Voters Representation against LLM Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32780v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI representation is emerging in digital democracy, assisting in voter emulation and decision-making. Large language models (LLMs) behave differently depending on the model, the prompt context, and the voter information they receive, representing some groups more accurately than others and potentially making the emulation unfair to certain groups. We investigate demographic biases in LLM-emulated electorates: how accurately LLMs represent individual votes, whether some voter groups are represented more accurately than others, and whether AI alignment can reduce these disparities without sacrificing overall accuracy. Using three U.S. presidential elections, we compare a closed-source and an open-source LLM across voters' demographic features and test AI alignment strategies toward fairer voter representation. Removing gender from the voter profile reduces the largest group error of the models by 22-42%, and removing education reduces it by 64%, whereas removing self-reported race increases the mean group error by over 40%, all without lowering accuracy. Common alignment strategies, such as repeated queries or fairness instructions, do not reduce these errors and can double them.</p></details> |  |
| **[Interchangeable in Role, Not in History: Replacing Members of Formed LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multiagent LLM systems often treat a role as a slot: when a member is replaced, any agent competent at the role is expected to fill it. We test this view on teams whose members keep short written notebooks. A one-seat swap replays a formed, frozen team with its own member, with a same-role newcomer carrying another team's notebook for that role, or with a newcomer carrying none. In cooperative cooking, wildfire response and medical question answering, the experienced newcomer, averaged over seats, outscores the one without a notebook in every setting, so the usual comparison would approve it. Against the original team, the same replacement falls short in every setting, and the team spends more messages per unit of progress. On the hardest cooking level, the shortfall appears only after the team has worked together, persists across model families and decoding temperatures, and narrows by two thirds with added planning-only members. Because the team's history is written down, we can take it apart. In the default configuration on that level, notebooks from the same donor teams leave a newcomer that leads, holding the recipe, worse off than no notebook and one that follows better off. The notebook lines about the partner reproduce this whole difference between the seats. Whether an agent can replace a teammate depends on the seat and the team it enters.</p></details> |  |
| **[Honeyquest for LLMs: Rethinking Cyber Deception for AI Attackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21037v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The empirical foundation of cyber deception relies on human-centered hypotheses, but the rapid emergence of autonomous, AI-enabled attackers challenges whether this foundation transfers to AI agents. To address this, we introduce an automated evaluation framework adapted from the Honeyquest instrument to assess LLM attacker judgment at scale. Our 21-LLM cohort spanned 10 providers, diverse architectures and specializations, open- and closed-weight models, and parameter scales from 8B to over 1T. We evaluated the performance of this LLM cohort (yielding 10,962 responses) against the 47-participant human baseline across an identical set of 174 reconnaissance queries. Our empirical evaluation reveals three key findings that establish LLMs as a distinct attacker class: (1) every model in our cohort falls for deceptive traps at a significantly higher rate than human attackers; (2) the defensive attention-diversion effect observed in humans is statistically absent in our LLM cohort; and (3) a critical recognition-action gap, where LLMs successfully articulate trap recognition in their reasoning but exploit the deceptive elements anyway 73.4% of the time. Across the 21 models, trap recognition in reasoning text did not predict fell-for-trap behavior (Spearman r = +0.08, p = 0.73). Ultimately, these findings demonstrate that human-centered deception hypotheses do not reliably transfer to AI attackers, highlighting the critical need for new research into AI-native active defense frameworks.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 4 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[ActionRAG: Evidence-Refining Graph Diffusion for Long-Term Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32752v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents need long-term memory that can recover precise and mutually consistent evidence from growing interaction histories, particularly when answers depend on relations among facts rather than isolated semantic matches. We introduce ActionRAG, a graph memory architecture that couples cautious indexing with query-time graph retroevaluation. Its indexing pipeline reconciles type labels and applies tiered entity resolution. Given a query, dual-channel entity and triple retrieval forms an initial evidence set and induces a bounded local graph. Query-conditioned receiver-normalized diffusion refines triple scores using local path structure. Seed protection, hub-penalized flux, and triple materialization preserve direct evidence while recovering missed connections. We evaluate ActionRAG on MemoryAgentBench (MAB), which tests accurate retrieval, conflict resolution, long-range understanding, and test-time learning. Across its eight deterministic-scoring columns, ActionRAG achieves 55.1 average accuracy, improving over the strongest retrieval-based memory baseline by 5.4 points. Under MAB's query-time cost accounting, mean synthesis cost stays below \$0.001 per query on the four reported cost columns, and the sum of separately measured retrieval-side p99 components is 440.7 ms on our consumer Apple Silicon setup.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Answer, Different Confidence: Protocol Sensitivity in LLM Confidence Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27752v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLM) can state their confidence in an answer (verbalized confidence), or the confidence can be read from the probability of the answer's tokens (token confidence). Comparisons of the two ask which is better calibrated, but the token probability of an answer depends on the text that precedes it. Across five open LLMs and four question-answering datasets, we fix each answer, its verbalized confidence, and its correctness label, and vary how each score is measured. The scores themselves change with the protocol, and so does the comparison between them. Changing the context in which the token probability is read shifts the gap between the two scores by about the size of the gap itself (.287 vs. .304 in expected calibration error, ECE; .189 vs. .145 in AUROC). The 95% interval of the shift excludes zero in 19 and 17 of the 20 settings, and under AUROC the shift has the same sign in all of them, so the context changes how answers are ranked. The better score flips in 8 settings under each metric, counting only flips that the intervals support, and the shift also appears in four larger models of up to 72B parameters. Most of the shift appears with changes to the instruction, not to the position of the answer. Other measurement choices also matter, and stated confidence depends on how a supplied answer is written. We turn these results into a reporting checklist for LLM confidence and its comparisons.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Butterfly Defects: Tracing Cooperation in LLM Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32726v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) increasingly operate as interconnected agents, a fundamental question emerges: does individual cooperation translate into collective cooperation? We investigate this question through the Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (IPD), moving from dyadic interactions to homogeneous and heterogeneous networks of open-weight LLMs. Our findings reveal that cooperation is highly sensitive to model composition and seemingly minor linguistic changes. Models that cooperate with identical peers can shift toward defection in mixed populations. A simple uncertainty cue can increase or decrease cooperation, redistribute costs, and affect unexposed agents several hops away. Even reversing action-label order can dramatically alter behavior, with uncertainty framing sometimes amplifying this sensitivity. Together, these findings challenge the notion of cooperation as a stable model-level property, revealing how strategic behavior depends on interaction partners, network positions, and the language used to frame decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Hotness: High-Resolution Memory Characterization of Cloud and LLM Workloads for Tiered Memory with ARM SPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32711v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory demands from data analytics and LLM workloads grow rapidly, driving up memory costs in cloud and data centers. This work introduces a high-resolution memory characterization methodology for analyzing workload-specific opportunities for multi-tier memory usage. In response to the increasing deployment of ARM-based processors in data centers, we implement our methodology using the recent Statistical Profiling Extension (SPE) for ARM. By characterizing eight memory-intensive workloads from CloudSuite and LLM applications, we identify region-specific access patterns, evaluate the effectiveness of cloud-oriented cache hierarchies, and propose new metrics based on memory idle periods and latency profiles to determine suitable data placement for tiered memory.</p></details> |  |
| **[SANE: Automated Scorer-Specific Metamorphic Testing for LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28886v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges increasingly mediate benchmark evaluation, model selection, data curation, and training. Their decisions are useful only when each judge responds reliably to changes affecting its scoring criterion. Existing methods for reliability testing perturb inputs with known expected effects on judge scores. Prominent LLM-judge testing harnesses combine human-authored perturbation recipes with synthetic test generation, leaving scorer-specific recipe ideation outside the automated pipeline. This limits their use to fixed perturbations and pre-assumed scorer types. We introduce SANE, an agent-based framework that encodes behavioral requirements for stochastic evaluators as reusable agent instructions. Given a scorer specification, implementation, and representative inputs, the pipeline first identifies scorer-specific capabilities, then automatically derives perturbation recipes and expected score relations for each. SANE also defines a scorer-independent validation framework for generated judge tests. It determines whether each recipe agrees with scorer intent and whether each instantiated case realizes the recipe and supports its expected relation. Because SANE generates a recipe inventory tailored to each scorer, the number of aligned recipes grows as scorers are added. Across five domains, SANE produces 3.7 times as many validated test families as the Judge Reliability Harness (JRH) at comparable end-to-end validity and detects scorer deficiencies in every domain, including professionalism and toxicity, where JRH detects none. On faithfulness, SANE achieves strong human alignment in judge ranking: its test-suite scores rank 11 judge models consistently with human-labeled FaithBench performance (Spearman rho = 0.852), while JRH does not (rho = 0.100).</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures. A shorter version was accepted for presentation at the non-archival NeurIPS 2026 TAE workshop</p></details> |
| **[InferSlice: A Reference Architecture for SLO-driven LLM Inference over a Telco Wide-Area Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32638v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM inference is increasingly deployed as a core service for enterprise and interactive applications, but sovereignty, compliance, latency and resource-locality requirements can limit the suitability of centralised cloud deployments. Telco operators offer geographically distributed compute and network infrastructure for addressing these constraints. However, existing LLM serving frameworks are designed for tightly coupled data centres and provide limited support for coordinating geographically distributed and heterogeneous resources. We present InferSlice, a reference architecture for Service-Level Objective (SLO)-driven LLM inference over a telco infrastructure, integrating network slicing, GPU partitioning, elastic serving and observability. Led by BT in collaboration with the University of Bristol and NVIDIA, and realised on the JOINER UK national-scale wide-area network (WAN), InferSlice is evaluated on service differentiation across tiers, elastic scale-out, client-edge bandwidth as a bottleneck, and the reach of tensor, pipeline, data parallelism, and prefill/decode disaggregation over the WAN. Our results show that, on a general-purpose telco network, tiers yield distinct latency-throughput regimes, and weight streaming shortens scale-out time. Replication is placement-insensitive but memory-bound; pipeline parallelism tolerates near- and mid-cross-site hops, while tensor parallelism and disaggregation fail beyond a single subnet hop. We share practical insights from operating SLO-driven inference on a telco network, lessons learned and open questions, and hope to encourage further research on distributed and heterogeneous LLM inference systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[LGMT: A Tool for Testing the Logical Reasoning Reliability of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32627v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly serve as reasoning components in modern software systems, yet a model that answers a logical query correctly can contradict itself when the same problem is restated in an equivalent form. Static benchmarks evaluate queries in isolation and therefore fail to expose such inconsistencies. To address this limitation, we present LGMT, an open-source tool that detects reasoning inconsistencies through metamorphic relations grounded in first-order logic. Given a source test case in premise-conclusion form, LGMT generates logically equivalent follow-up cases and checks whether the model's entailment predictions agree across the pair, without ground-truth labels. The tool provides 20 built-in metamorphic relations, supports custom ones verified by the Z3 theorem prover, and runs from a CLI or a web studio, including an offline mock mode requiring no API key. Across six widely used LLMs on a bundled benchmark of 3,091 metamorphic groups, LGMT reveals inconsistency rates ranging from 20.84% to 29.44%. LGMT is publicly available on GitHub at https://github.com/MaxwelsDonc/lgmt-tool, with a demonstration video at https://youtu.be/gou4opptu1U.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Uncertain Judgments to Calibrated Rankings: Conformal Elo Estimation for LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13221v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating new large language models typically requires costly human annotation campaigns at scale. | **[Political sense-making in "individual" human-technology-world relations: the case of LLM-generated autobiographies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32594v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI systems increasingly mediate how people describe, recognize, and present themselves, their politics cannot be reduced to what remains hidden inside the "black box." When users ask systems such as ChatGPT to describe them, they do not simply receive algorithmic representations. They interpret, contest, edit, share, and publicly test these descriptions. In doing so, people move interpretively among affective resonance, friction, public re-articulation, and the sedimentation of meaning. This means that the so-called "micro-level" of postphenomenological analysis is already saturated with publicity: affective uptake becomes communicable, reflection becomes accountable, and uses sediment into shared standards of appearance. Simultaneously, we show that once mediation is treated as relational and temporal, the political is not an add-on at the "macro" level; it is intrinsic to the very movement through which technologies amplify and reduce, stabilize and unsettle, and thereby compose a shared world in which judgment can take place. (in press). "Political sense-making in 'individual' human-technology-world relations: the case of LLM-generated autobiographies." In D. Michelfelder, & J. Kennedy (Eds.), Postphenomenology Meets Artificial Intelligence. Bloomsbury Academic</p></details> |

| **[Democratizing LLM Training with the Edge: Challenges, Systems, and the Road Ahead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32215v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growing computational power of modern GPU data centers has let a few industrial giants train and deploy large language models (LLMs) on massive amounts of data. | **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. As a result, AI accelerators achieve varying levels of efficiency across the two phases. GPUs are well suited to prefill, but are often underutilized during decode. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. HAWK establishes a remote direct memory access (RDMA) path for direct cross-device transfers while handling inter-device tensor layout discrepancies as part of the transfer process. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. Across four domains and 20 LLMs, DIVA-U has lower regret, i.e., value lost relative to the best possible decision, than every LLM baseline on real-world data, mainly by stopping once evidence suffices, and DIVA cuts the regret of the best LLM baseline by 75% in synthetic scenarios where every check's reliability is given. | **[Are LLM-Generated Formal Specifications Faithful to Their Requirements? Our study reaches several interesting findings: first, when the code implementation is correct, providing the natural language requirement does not further improve the pass rate of generated specifications. We developed automated assessment pipelines using large language models (LLMs) to measure therapist behaviors (empathy, exploration), relational quality (rapport), and client outcomes (self-disclosure, self-directed negative emotion, outward-directed negative emotion). Model-generated scores were validated against human annotations. We then applied these measures to approximately 2,000 hours of psychotherapy transcripts from the Alexander Street corpus. This yielded an estimation sample of 119,233 client utterances from 1,573 sessions and 37 clients. Automated measures showed good to excellent agreement with human annotations (ICC = 0.60 - 0.81), with rapport (ICC = 0.81), self-disclosure (ICC = 0.78), empathy components (ICC = 0.60 - 0.75), and emotion measurements (ICC = 0.66 - 0.80). Both behaviors were also associated with reduced expressions of enjoyment. Surprisingly, rapport in the prior session showed no association with client self-disclosure or negative emotion expression. | **[AgentPhishing: A Benchmark of LLM Email Agents Under Adaptive Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31404v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly handle private information and act on users' behalf across desktop and web applications, including email. To expose vulnerabilities that pre-existing datasets may miss, we introduce AgentPhishing, a benchmark for evaluating whether persuasive phishing attacks can evade detection and induce LLM email agents to comply with malicious requests. We develop PhishOpt, which optimizes reusable email-generation skills against individual agents under fixed benchmark constraints and a realism constraint. We analyze the generated emails using Cialdini's persuasion principles to identify strategies associated with agent failures and use these findings to construct a universal generation skill. Our evaluation shows that agents can handle pre-existing phishing emails safely yet remain susceptible to attacks adapted to agent behavior, while in an AgentPhishing evaluation of nine agents, four exhibit phishing-detection rates below $10\%$ and malicious-request compliance rates above $45\%$. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and seven search-augmented question-answering benchmarks show consistent improvements over the evaluated baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, TRCA improves the WebShop score by 6.0%-12.6%; with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, it improves the average SearchQA score by 1.9%-18.3%. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. | **[SARIA: Persistence-Aware KV Caching with Coordinated DRAM--SSD Reload for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31169v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents usually reuse long context prefixes, making key value (KV) caching crucial for reducing prefill latency. On the read path, \textsc{Saria} promotes selected SSD-hit KV cache blocks from GPU memory into DRAM after prefill, allowing subsequent reuse within the same session to reduce SSD read traffic while limiting DRAM contention. A transfer coordinator paces DRAM reads while SSD transfers are active, allowing both paths to make progress and efficiently share GPU ingress bandwidth. | **[ToolRACER: A Robust Agentic Conversation Emulation Resource for Agent Training and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09163v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Task-oriented conversational agents remain fragile under real world conversation scenarios as they rarely follow a predictable script, especially when users exhibit non-cooperative behavior. Existing function-calling benchmarks often emphasize successful, cooperative interactions and underrepresent adversarial conversation trajectories, thereby limiting the training resources available for developing robust agents. We present ToolRACER, a synthetic data generation pipeline that coordinates user, assistant and tool emulation models to generate and validated multi-turn interactions between a user and an agent. Using ToolRACER, we construct ToolRACERBench a robust multi-turn conversation benchmark spanning six domains, ranging over 55 varied personas, generating a validated corpus of 5.6K conversation trajectories, with approximately 66\% of conversations containing failure-prone conversation scenarios. We inject adversarial behaviors, producing validated conversational interaction trajectories that capture realistic, robust scenarios. We evaluate models trained on ToolRACERBench against internal benchmarks, as well as on function calling benchmarks such as $τ^2$-bench, BFCLv3 and ACEBench to evaluate agentic accuracy and robustness. Models trained on ToolRACERBench improve end to end agentic accuracy across $τ^2$-bench and ACEBench, demonstrating significant gains when mixed with in-domain dataset in small language models for agent capability tasks.</p></details> |  |

We extend FunctionGemma 270M-it to practical Android workflows by introducing MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, a synthetic, schema-validated dataset of ~9,500 conversations covering fifteen device-control categories, including messaging, phone calls, camera/screenshot, brightness control, device-status queries, flashlight control, and application management. We fine-tune the 270M model with TRL supervised fine-tuning under completion-only loss, producing an extended specialist and a combined model trained jointly with Google's MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE. On MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, end-to-end accuracy improves from 29.3% for the base model and 17.2% for Google's Mobile-Actions variant to 76.5%. The combined model retains 76.5% on MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED and reaches 82.3% on MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE, down from the 90.3% of Google's Mobile-Actions specialist, representing an 8.0-percentage-point trade-off in return for doubling category coverage. We release the dataset, fine-tuned models, reproducible training/evaluation pipeline, and an Android demo, highlighting compact local function calling as a practical path towards low-latency and privacy-preserving mobile assistants.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026. 16 pages, 1 figure, 8 tables. Includes main paper, references, and appendices. Code, datasets, models, and demo available at GitHub and Hugging Face</p></details> |
| **[Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03966v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-use evaluations typically measure calls through a serving stack, which can report zero calls even when model outputs contain call-shaped payloads. Full-return direct regression reverses an initially favorable doubly robust (DR) evaluation result in the linear setting: mean absolute errors are 0.0139 for direct regression and 0.0272 for DR. Under a shifted environment, DR retains an advantage, with errors 0.0227 versus 0.0948. On function-name-group-disjoint BFCL-derived splits, direct and DR selectors obtain balanced accuracies of 81.85% and 79.83%. Two pinned local Qwen2.5 models are evaluated on the same 200 held-out tasks, exposing a strong failure to abstain under the fixed prompt. We further characterize policy differences under missing support: unsupported actions shared by two policies cancel, allowing point identification of an incremental change when neither absolute value is identifiable. A disagreement-preserving fallback achieves this property in all five support-gap runs, but conservative sampling bounds do not certify deployment improvement. The contribution is a falsifiable evaluation method and independent public evidence, not a new DR estimator, official BFCL leaderboard score, or production-agent safety claim.</p></details> | <details><summary>Worki...</summary><p>Working paper, version 2. | **[UFO: Chain-of-Evaluation for Omni-Condition Alignment in Multi-Modal Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12397v2)** | 2026-09-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-modal image generation, particularly subject-driven customization, has garnered growing attention in recent years. Empirical evaluations confirm the effectiveness of our data-centric methodology, with which classification performs better under distribution shift compared to model-based approaches, and consistently further enhances robustness when used in conjunction. | **[EchoFuzz: Empowering Smart Contract Fuzzing with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14475v1)** | 2026-09-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Smart contracts, serving as the cornerstone of decentralized applications, autonomously manage trillion-dollar digital assets, making them attractive targets for attacks. (1) The logical gap in state transitions and combinatorial redundancy hinders effective tradeoffs between bug detection efficiency and state space exploration cost, leading to critical execution paths to be overlooked. (2) Rule-based sequence mutation strategies suffer from path redundancy and inadequate guidance from contract logic, resulting in performance bottlenecks that stall the exploration of in-depth vulnerability-oriented paths. To tackle these challenges, we propose EchoFuzz, an LLM-guided fuzzing framework introducing Vulnerable Function Call Sequences (VFCS) - minimal, behavior-preserving execution paths that expose bugs through key state transitions. EchoFuzz consists of two key procedures. First, we develop a chain-guided LLM approach, that combines static analysis with logical understanding to generate contract-specific VFCS candidates that eliminate combinatorial redundancy. Second, we adopt an iterative fuzzing strategy that uses LLMs with real-time feedback to adaptively steer fuzzer toward uncovered branches. Experiments show EchoFuzz outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving 29\% higher branch coverage and detecting 62\% more vulnerabilities. We present the system's architecture and report results from a comparative evaluation conducted with energy domain specialists, contrasting the Explainability Assistant with a traditional XAI dashboard. | **[AgentServeSim: Serving-System Simulation and Policy Search for LLM Agent Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09613v3)** | 2026-09-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents execute programs comprising multiple model turns interleaved with external tool calls. They therefore cannot jointly represent the cross-turn state and policy-dependent successor releases needed to evaluate counterfactual agent-serving trajectories. We present AgentServeSim, a simulator whose unit of execution is the agent program. A Program Control Block maintains cross-turn state, while a Program Orchestrator causally releases successor turns from simulated predecessor completions. A Retention Plane controls KV state across tool gaps, and a Dispatch Plane determines where and when each ready turn executes. We validate AgentServeSim against real vLLM deployments in 20 paired simulator-real cells spanning two GPU platforms, Llama-3.1-8B and Llama-3.1-70B, coding and function-calling agents, and five arrival rates. Mean JCT error remains within 5.5% on B200 and 5.2% in the saturated RTX PRO 6000 regime. Finally, we propose LLM-driven automated agent-serving policy search using AgentServeSim as a CPU-based fitness evaluator. | **[Repair, Not Improvement: Decomposing Constrained Decoding in Tool-Call Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13959v2)** | 2026-09-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A tool-calling router has to pick the right tool when one applies and decline when none does. Restricting the decoder to a grammar over the tool names is the standard remedy for the first, and on small models it buys a large accuracy gain. Recent work separates the loss caused by asking for a format from the loss caused by enforcing it at decode time. The second is small, which made enforcement look nearly free. The same work declines to extend that to function calling, where a constraint decides which answers exist rather than how one is written. Declining to call anything is the answer it most easily removes, and the one a router can least afford to lose. A grammar decides which tokens may be emitted and where generation stops, and a two-condition design charges both to the restriction. We therefore run three conditions over one prompt: free generation, generation stopped at the first line, and both applied together. We evaluate open-weight models from 0.6B to 4B on the same items in English and Korean, comparing the languages item by item. The two-condition contrast is negative on abstention accuracy in four of six cells with intervals excluding zero, and positive in none, costing -29.5 points at worst. On the smallest model in Korean the stop costs -20.0 points, the restriction returns +19.5, and together they leave -0.5. What the restriction gives back is readable output, not judgment. Of the 698 abstentions it repairs, 545 had no readable answer at all and 0 were correct decisions the scoring rule rejected. On items that do need a tool the contrast is positive throughout, and abstention is reported first because it is the registered measure. Both preregistered claims about language fail: Korean does not lose more of the abstentions it holds without the constraint, and the removed mass does not explain what does.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 4 figures, 21 tables</p></details> |

The log-normal weight structure and learnable q-parameter of Stieltjes-Wigert polynomials provide a distinct inductive bias that enables robust performance under resource-constrained conditions, including reduced feature dimensionality and limited training data. The proposed architecture not only outperforms established polynomial KAN baselines on standard benchmarks but also exhibits strong representational capacity for approximating complex multivariate functions with remarkably few parameters, making it a compelling alternative for efficient function approximation and classification in resource-constrained settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, Code and pretrained models available at: https://github.com/amirhoseinazarpour/SW-KAN</p></details> |

An LLM judge anchored to gold annotations classifies failures as missed calls, spurious calls, or parameter errors, producing a detailed error profile for each model. We evaluate five model families, including proprietary models and models with open weights, analyze their performance across difficulty levels and error categories, and validate the judge through agreement with human annotations.</p></details> |  |
| **[LogicIF: Towards Complex Logic Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09125v4)** | 2026-08-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Instruction following has catalyzed the recent era of Large Language Models (LLMs) and is the foundational skill underpinning more advanced capabilities such as reasoning and agentic behaviors. As tasks grow more challenging, the logic structures embedded in natural language instructions becomes increasingly intricate. However, how well LLMs perform on such logic-intensive instructions remains under-explored. We propose Logic Instruction Following (LogicIF), a task requiring models to precisely follow and simulate every logic step within complex instructions. To generate these instructions, we construct LogicIFGen, a scalable, automated framework for generating verifiable instructions from code functions, which can naturally express rich logic such as conditions, loops, nesting, and function calls. Using this framework, we curate a collection of complex code functions to construct LogicIFEval, a benchmark comprising 426 verifiable, human-examined, logic-rich instructions. Our experiments demonstrate that current state-of-the-art LLMs still struggle to correctly follow the instructions in LogicIFEval. Most LLMs can only follow fewer than 60% of the instructions, revealing significant deficiencies in their capacity to handle instructions that involve complex logic structures. To further demonstrate the efficacy of our framework, we collect an extensive set of code functions across a wide range of logic difficulties to generate LogicIFTrain, a fully synthesized training set featuring 27,324 verifiable instructions. Our results show that a model trained on LogicIFTrain using reinforcement learning significantly outperforms the base model on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks. All the data, codes and models are released at https://github.com/mianzhang/LogicIF.</p></details> | COLM 2026 Acceptance |
| **[An Agentic AI Framework Overcomes Fundamental Limitations of Large Language Models for Glaucoma Detection from Fundus Photography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07651v1)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) show promise in medical image interpretation but suffer from hallucination, limited accuracy, and run-to-run inconsistency. We developed and validated an agentic AI framework integrating LLMs with specialized deep learning tools for glaucoma detection from fundus photography. The workflow had three steps: (1) LLM initial assessment; (2) function calling to invoke specialized tools for image quality (QAModel, FundaQ-8), glaucoma classification (SwinV2-Tiny), and optic disc/cup segmentation (SegFormer-B0); and (3) LLM reflection integrating the initial impression with tool outputs. Two LLMs (Gemini 2.5 Flash, GPT-5.4 mini) were evaluated on two public datasets (ORIGA, n=100; RIM-ONE-v3, n=100) under uncropped and cropped fields of view; all images were independently graded by a masked fellowship-trained glaucoma specialist. The agentic workflow improved classification accuracy by 16 to 47 percentage points across all conditions, reaching within 6 points of the specialist; on RIM-ONE-v3 the best configurations matched the specialist accuracy of 88%. LLM-alone approaches failed in two ways: GPT-5.4 mini showed positive bias (sensitivity 95-100%, specificity 0-5%), while Gemini 2.5 Flash varied stochastically between runs; the agentic workflow corrected both. Cup-to-disc ratio error fell 15-50% (MAE 0.156-0.228 to 0.104-0.132), and correlation with specialist grading rose from weak (r=0.12-0.39) to moderate-strong (r=0.59-0.84). Run-to-run consistency rose from near-random (kappa as low as -0.01) to near-perfect (kappa up to 0.96). Integrating LLMs with specialized tools addressed key limitations of LLM-alone approaches, including over-diagnosis and run-to-run variability. Gains held for both LLMs, suggesting generalizability across backbones, and may signal a shift from monolithic models toward orchestrated multi-agent systems in medical AI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent Memory Distillation: Empowering Small LLM Agents with Hierarchical Teacher Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07169v1)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory systems have shown promise for improving agent performance, but their potential remains largely unexplored for small language models, which struggle to generate sufficient successful trajectories on their own. We propose Agent Memory Distillation (AMD), a training-free framework that transfers structured knowledge from a large teacher agent to a small student agent through hierarchical memory. AMD constructs three complementary memory types from successful teacher trajectories: Workflow memory encodes task-level strategies, Subtask memory provides concrete behavioral examples at an intermediate granularity, and Function memory captures per-function calling conventions and common pitfalls. Workflow and Subtask memories are injected proactively at the start of each task, while Function memory is retrieved reactively upon tool-calling errors. We evaluate AMD on three tool-use benchmarks using four student models (4B-8B parameters) with GPT-5-mini as the teacher, achieving average accuracy gains of 27.2%p, 11.2%p, and 3.4%p on AppWorld, BFCL V3, and ToolSandbox, while consistently outperforming existing memory-based baselines. Further analysis shows that Subtask memory contributes the largest gains, teacher effectiveness depends on both teacher capability and student compatibility, and 4B-sized students benefit most from AMD.</p></details> | Under review |

## LLM Tabular
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Instruction Tuning of Large Language Models for Tabular Data Generation-in One Day](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23220v1)** | 2025-11-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tabular instruction tuning has emerged as a promising research direction for improving LLMs understanding of tabular data. However, the majority of existing works only consider question-answering and reasoning tasks over tabular data, leaving tabular data generation largely unnoticed. In this work, for the first time, we explore the efficacy of instruction tuning in improving LLMs tabular data generation capabilities. More specifically, given the high data and computation requirements of tabular instruction tuning, we aim to address the possibility of instruction tuning for tabular data generation with limited data and computational resources. To achieve this, we first create a high-quality instruction dataset for tabular data, enabling efficient LLM comprehension. We then instruction-tune an open-source LLM (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct) on the training set of this dataset to improve its tabular data generation performance. Our experimental results show that by using our high-quality dataset and instruction-tuning on only 7K instructions with an A100 GPU, for less than 6 hours, we achieve tabular data generation performance on par with the most capable commercial LLM, GPT-4o.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2025), 1st Workshop on Foundation Models for Structured Data</p></details> |
| **[Small Models are LLM Knowledge Triggers on Medical Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.01570v3)** | 2025-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent development in large language models (LLMs) has demonstrated impressive domain proficiency on unstructured textual or multi-modal tasks. However, despite with intrinsic world knowledge, their application on structured tabular data prediction still lags behind, primarily due to the numerical insensitivity and modality discrepancy that brings a gap between LLM reasoning and statistical tabular learning. Unlike textual or vision data (e.g., electronic clinical notes or medical imaging data), tabular data is often presented in heterogeneous numerical values (e.g., CBC reports). This ubiquitous data format requires intensive expert annotation, and its numerical nature limits LLMs' capability to effectively transfer untapped domain expertise. In this paper, we propose SERSAL, a general self-prompting method by synergy learning with small models to enhance LLM tabular prediction in an unsupervised manner. Specifically, SERSAL utilizes the LLM's prior outcomes as original soft noisy annotations, which are dynamically leveraged to teach a better small student model. Reversely, the outcomes from the trained small model are used to teach the LLM to further refine its real capability. This process can be repeatedly applied to gradually distill refined knowledge for continuous progress. Comprehensive experiments on widely used medical domain tabular datasets show that, without access to gold labels, applying SERSAL to OpenAI GPT reasoning process attains substantial improvement compared to linguistic prompting methods, which serves as an orthogonal direction for tabular LLM, and increasing prompting bonus is observed as more powerful LLMs appear.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICLR 2025. Codes will be available at https://github.com/jyansir/sersal</p></details> |
| **[Elephants Never Forget: Memorization and Learning of Tabular Data in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06209v3)** | 2024-12-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While many have shown how Large Language Models (LLMs) can be applied to a diverse set of tasks, the critical issues of data contamination and memorization are often glossed over. In this work, we address this concern for tabular data. Specifically, we introduce a variety of different techniques to assess whether a language model has seen a tabular dataset during training. This investigation reveals that LLMs have memorized many popular tabular datasets verbatim. We then compare the few-shot learning performance of LLMs on datasets that were seen during training to the performance on datasets released after training. We find that LLMs perform better on datasets seen during training, indicating that memorization leads to overfitting. At the same time, LLMs show non-trivial performance on novel datasets and are surprisingly robust to data transformations. We then investigate the in-context statistical learning abilities of LLMs. While LLMs are significantly better than random at solving statistical classification problems, the sample efficiency of few-shot learning lags behind traditional statistical learning algorithms, especially as the dimension of the problem increases. This suggests that much of the observed few-shot performance on novel real-world datasets is due to the LLM's world knowledge. Overall, our results highlight the importance of testing whether an LLM has seen an evaluation dataset during pre-training. We release the https://github.com/interpretml/LLM-Tabular-Memorization-Checker Python package to test LLMs for memorization of tabular datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>COLM ...</summary><p>COLM camera ready, fix typo</p></details> |
| **[LLM Embeddings Improve Test-time Adaptation to Tabular $Y|X$-Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07395v1)** | 2024-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For tabular datasets, the change in the relationship between the label and covariates ($Y|X$-shifts) is common due to missing variables (a.k.a. confounders). Since it is impossible to generalize to a completely new and unknown domain, we study models that are easy to adapt to the target domain even with few labeled examples. We focus on building more informative representations of tabular data that can mitigate $Y|X$-shifts, and propose to leverage the prior world knowledge in LLMs by serializing (write down) the tabular data to encode it. We find LLM embeddings alone provide inconsistent improvements in robustness, but models trained on them can be well adapted/finetuned to the target domain even using 32 labeled observations. Our finding is based on a comprehensive and systematic study consisting of 7650 source-target pairs and benchmark against 261,000 model configurations trained by 22 algorithms. Our observation holds when ablating the size of accessible target data and different adaptation strategies. The code is available at https://github.com/namkoong-lab/LLM-Tabular-Shifts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Elephants Never Forget: Testing Language Models for Memorization of Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.06644v1)** | 2024-03-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While many have shown how Large Language Models (LLMs) can be applied to a diverse set of tasks, the critical issues of data contamination and memorization are often glossed over. In this work, we address this concern for tabular data. Starting with simple qualitative tests for whether an LLM knows the names and values of features, we introduce a variety of different techniques to assess the degrees of contamination, including statistical tests for conditional distribution modeling and four tests that identify memorization. Our investigation reveals that LLMs are pre-trained on many popular tabular datasets. This exposure can lead to invalid performance evaluation on downstream tasks because the LLMs have, in effect, been fit to the test set. Interestingly, we also identify a regime where the language model reproduces important statistics of the data, but fails to reproduce the dataset verbatim. On these datasets, although seen during training, good performance on downstream tasks might not be due to overfitting. Our findings underscore the need for ensuring data integrity in machine learning tasks with LLMs. To facilitate future research, we release an open-source tool that can perform various tests for memorization \url{https://github.com/interpretml/LLM-Tabular-Memorization-Checker}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Table...</summary><p>Table Representation Learning Workshop at NeurIPS 2023</p></details> |
